> **A:** Spreading activation is the mechanism by which activating one concept node in associative memory automatically propagates along weighted links to neighboring nodes, partially activating them. It operates pre-consciously and enables expert fluency. It matters for PKB design because a knowledge graph with richly typed, dense links creates a structural substrate that partially externalizes the activation pathway network — though the practitioner must manually traverse it, the structure facilitates the same conceptual neighborhood access that spreading activation provides internally.
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Source:** Section 3: Schema-Driven Processing
> **Tags:** `spreading-activation` `PKB-architecture` `semantic-memory`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Bidirectional Enrichment Hypothesis?
> **A:** The BiEH proposes a compounding feedback cycle between PKB use (external) and internal schema development: (Outside-in) Building skeleton frameworks, elaborating links, and writing schemas for the PKB requires self-explanation, which strengthens internal schema organization. (Inside-out) Richer internal schemas enable more structurally accurate and associatively dense PKB construction. Both directions feed each other, generating compounding returns for practitioners who maintain the cycle.
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Source:** Section 7: Bidirectional Enrichment
> **Tags:** `bidirectional-enrichment` `PKB-theory` `schema-development` `original-synthesis`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Describe the Parity Principle (Clark & Chalmers) and its relevance to PKB design.
> **A:** The Parity Principle: if an external process plays the same functional role that an internal cognitive process would play, it is rightfully classified as part of the cognitive system. Relevance to PKB: if a PKB participates in schema-driven processing in the same functional role that schematic LTM would, it qualifies as cognitive extension. However, the principle requires the use conditions to be met (reliable availability, accessible endorsement, habitually trusted); the schema externalization thesis doesn't require the full metaphysical claim, only that structural correspondence improves coupling quality and functional contribution.
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Source:** Section 5: The Extended Mind Thesis
> **Tags:** `parity-principle` `extended-mind` `cognitive-extension` `Andy-Clark`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes intrinsic, extraneous, and germane cognitive load?
> **A:** Intrinsic load: inherent complexity of the material, determined by element interactivity — cannot be reduced except by building schemas first. Extraneous load: unnecessary cognitive work from poor presentation design — should be minimized. Germane load: cognitive effort directed at schema construction and elaboration — should be optimized (not maximized). A PKB supports learning by reducing extraneous load (outsourcing reference material) and freeing capacity for germane load (active schema building), NOT by reducing intrinsic load, which remains constant.
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Source:** Section 4: Working Memory as the Bottleneck
> **Tags:** `cognitive-load` `CLT` `working-memory` `schema-construction`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Three-Register PKB framework?
> **A:** The Three-Register PKB proposes that an effective schema-fidelity PKB serves three functional registers simultaneously: (1) Foundation — permanent notes capturing concept-level schema nodes for long-term stability; (2) Synthesis — MOCs and hub notes articulating relationships and hierarchical structure among schema nodes; (3) Laboratory — ephemeral working notes, active reading annotations, and analytical drafts capturing the current knowledge-work process. The three registers correspond to semantic memory (stable concepts), schema structure (relations and hierarchies), and working memory support (active processing).
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Source:** Synthesis and Integration
> **Tags:** `three-register-PKB` `PKB-architecture` `original-synthesis`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Why does the encoding specificity principle create a challenge for PKB practitioners?
> **A:** Encoding specificity (Tulving & Thomson): retrieval is most effective when retrieval cues match the encoding context. The challenge: when we later search the PKB from a different conceptual context than the one in which we created a note, retrieval may fail even when the note is relevant — because the retrieval cue doesn't match the conceptual context embedded in the note's creation. Schema-fidelity design addresses this by creating multiple access pathways to each concept (multiple links from different contexts), reducing dependence on any single encoding context for retrieval.
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Source:** Section 3: Schema-Driven Processing
> **Tags:** `encoding-specificity` `retrieval` `PKB-design` `Tulving`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What does expertise research (Chase & Simon, 1973) reveal about chunking and schemas?
> **A:** Chase & Simon's chessboard studies showed that expert chess players can reconstruct 20+ piece positions from 5-second viewing while novices reconstruct only 4–6 pieces. The advantage disappears with randomly arranged pieces. This reveals that experts don't have more working memory capacity — they have richer schemas: they perceive meaningful configurations (attacks, defenses, standard positions) as single chunks, each representing 5–7 individual pieces. The key insight for PKBs: chunking multiplies information density per working memory slot, and chunking capacity is entirely dependent on schema richness.
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Source:** Section 2: The Architecture of Schemas in Long-Term Memory
> **Tags:** `chunking` `expertise` `schemas` `working-memory` `Chase-and-Simon`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the testing effect, and why does it produce better retention than re-studying?
> **A:** The testing effect is the finding that retrieving information from long-term memory produces stronger, more durable traces than equivalent re-studying. It works because: (1) retrieval builds the retrieval pathway specifically; (2) elaborative retrieval enriches encoding; (3) failed retrieval creates error-correction opportunities; (4) it is a desirable difficulty that engages consolidation processes.
> **Source:** Section 4, [[Testing-Effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fluency illusion, and why is it a metacognitive problem?
> **A:** The fluency illusion is using processing ease (how smoothly text is read) as a cue for comprehension depth (how deeply it has been understood). It is a metacognitive monitoring error: the wrong signal (fluency) is feeding the judgment-of-learning system, producing systematic overconfidence in understanding and premature termination of study.
> **Source:** Sections 1 and 7, [[Fluency-Illusion]]
> **Difficulty:** Basic

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is a desirable difficulty, and what distinguishes it from undesirable difficulty?
> **A:** A desirable difficulty impedes performance during acquisition but produces better long-term retention and transfer by engaging constructive and reconstructive memory operations (retrieval, elaboration, discrimination). Undesirable difficulty impedes performance *without* engaging these operations — e.g., cramped fonts, poor organization, cognitive overload beyond competence zone.
> **Source:** Section 2, [[Desirable-Difficulties]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** How do spacing and retrieval practice interact, and why is the interaction important?
> **A:** Retrieval practice is substantially more powerful when combined with spacing: testing at a delay requires actual retrieval from long-term memory (implicit retrieval practice embedded in spaced study), while testing immediately draws on working memory. The combination — retrieval at optimally spaced intervals — is the theoretical foundation of spaced repetition systems.
> **Source:** Sections 4 and 5, [[Spaced-Repetition]], [[Retrieval-Practice]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the interleaving effect, and why does it feel less productive than blocked study?
> **A:** The interleaving effect is that mixing problem types or topics within a study session produces better long-term retention and transfer than studying each type exhaustively before moving to the next. It feels less productive because it prevents the shallow repetition fluency that blocked study generates — requiring re-encoding from scratch on each encounter, which is the source of both the felt difficulty and the learning benefit.
> **Source:** Section 5, [[Interleaving-Effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** According to Self-Determination Theory, what are the three basic psychological needs and their relevance to academic reading engagement?
> **A:** Autonomy (experiencing reading as self-chosen, identity-aligned), Competence (experiencing genuine mastery growth through challenge calibrated to current ability), and Relatedness (experiencing connection to intellectual community and purpose). All three must be satisfied for reading engagement to be sustainable and autonomously motivated rather than compliance-driven and fragile.
> **Source:** Section 8, [[Basic-Psychological-Needs]], [[Self-Determination-Theory]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the generation effect, and how does it differ from retrieval practice?
> **A:** The generation effect is the finding that self-generated information is encoded more durably than received information. Unlike retrieval practice (which retrieves already-encoded information), generation occurs *during initial encoding* — before durable storage. Both engage active construction, but at different points in the learning cycle: generation at encoding, retrieval practice during maintenance.
> **Source:** Section 3, [[Generation-Effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the self-explanation effect, and what is its primary mechanism?
> **A:** Students who generate more self-explanations during problem-solving and text study (articulating why claims are true, how processes work) show substantially greater learning gains than those who explain less. The primary mechanism is gap-detection: self-explanation forces the learner to confront logical gaps in their understanding that fluent reading conceals, triggering targeted repair of exactly the incomplete aspects of their mental model.
> **Source:** Section 6, [[Self-Explanation-Effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three phases of Zimmerman's cyclical self-regulated learning model, and what is their relevance to strategic reading?
> **A:** Forethought (goal-setting, prior knowledge activation, strategy planning before reading), Performance (active strategy deployment with comprehension monitoring during reading), and Self-Reflection (honest post-reading evaluation of what was learned and strategy effectiveness). Most natural readers skip forethought and self-reflection entirely, which eliminates the cyclical improvement mechanism that makes SRL more effective over time.
> **Source:** Section 10, [[Cyclical-Model-of-Self-Regulated-Learning]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** In the Nelson-Narens model of metacognition, what are monitoring and control, and how do they interact?
> **A:** Monitoring: information flowing from the object (cognitive) level to the meta level, including judgments of learning, feelings of knowing, and comprehension signals. Control: commands flowing from the meta level to the object level, adjusting strategies, allocating study time, deciding to review. Effective self-regulation requires both: monitoring that generates accurate signals, and control operations that respond to those signals adaptively.
> **Source:** Section 7, [[Nelson-Narens-Model]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 1
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four components of Baddeley's working memory model?
> **A:** The phonological loop (verbal/acoustic storage), visuospatial sketchpad (visual/spatial processing), episodic buffer (integration workspace), and central executive (attentional control and coordination).
> **Source:** Section 1.1
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #working-memory #cognitive-architecture

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 2
> **A:** **Q:** What is the current best estimate for working memory capacity in the focus of attention?
> **A:** Approximately 3-5 items, with 4 as the modal estimate (Cowan, 2001) — significantly lower than Miller's classic 7±2.
> **Source:** Section 1.2
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #working-memory #capacity-limits

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 3
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three types of cognitive load in Sweller's Cognitive Load Theory, and how do they compete?
> **A:** Intrinsic load (from material complexity/element interactivity), extraneous load (from poor design or environment), and germane load (productive schema-building effort). They are additive and cannot exceed total WM capacity — any increase in extraneous load subtracts from germane processing.
> **Source:** Section 2.1–2.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #cognitive-load-theory #instructional-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 4
> **A:** **Q:** What does Wickens' Multiple Resource Theory predict about walking while reading?
> **A:** Relatively LOW interference, because walking is primarily motor-spatial-ambient and reading is primarily cognitive-verbal-focal. They overlap mainly on central executive coordination, but use largely distinct resource pools.
> **Source:** Section 3.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #multiple-resource-theory #dual-task

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 5
> **A:** **Q:** What key finding from Oppezzo & Schwartz (2014) is commonly misinterpreted regarding walking and cognition?
> **A:** Walking enhanced *divergent* (creative) thinking by ~60%, but it slightly *impaired* convergent (analytical) thinking. Reading comprehension is convergent — so the study actually argues against walking while reading complex analytical material.
> **Source:** Section 5.2
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #embodied-cognition #creativity #convergent-vs-divergent

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 6
> **A:** **Q:** What is the "timing asymmetry" in the exercise-cognition literature, and what does it imply for reading?
> **A:** Exercise during complex cognitive tasks shows null-to-negative effects, while exercise before complex tasks shows reliably positive effects (lasting 30-60 minutes post-exercise). Implication: walk BEFORE reading, not during.
> **Source:** Section 6.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #exercise-cognition #yerkes-dodson

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 7
> **A:** **Q:** What is the generation effect, and why does it make the walking-reading question practically irrelevant?
> **A:** The generation effect (Slamecka & Graf, 1978) shows that actively producing information yields d = 0.40–0.87 better retention than passive reception. Walking-reading is inherently passive (no annotation, summarization, or PKB processing possible). The opportunity cost of losing the generation effect (~40-80% retention benefit) vastly exceeds walking's modest dual-task cost (~10-25%).
> **Source:** Section 7.1, 7.3
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #generation-effect #opportunity-cost #active-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 8
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three conditions under which walking-reading remains defensible according to this report?
> **A:** (1) Material you already know well (low intrinsic load due to developed schemas), (2) low element-interactivity material (narrative, biographical), and (3) audio-first formats (eliminates gaze-stabilization and device-management costs).
> **Source:** Section 8.5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #practical-strategy #walking-reading

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 9
> **A:** **Q:** Why is automaticity better understood as a continuum than a binary, and what does this mean for walking while reading?
> **A:** Moors & De Houwer (2006) argue that automaticity features (efficiency, awareness, intentionality, controllability) are partially independent — a process can be automatic on some features but not others. Walking is efficient and low-awareness but requires ongoing balance monitoring, turning decisions, and gaze stabilization. These residual demands fall on a working memory system with no spare capacity during complex reading.
> **Source:** Section 4.1–4.2
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #automaticity #graded-automaticity #dual-task

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 10
> **A:** **Q:** What is the "Walk-Read-Walk Sandwich" protocol, and what cognitive mechanism does each phase exploit?
> **A:** (1) Pre-walk (15-20 min): schema activation, arousal priming via exercise-cognition effects. (2) Desk reading (45-60 min): deep comprehension and generation effect through active PKB engagement. (3) Post-walk (15-20 min): memory consolidation, retrieval practice, and creative incubation through embodied cognition effects.
> **Source:** Section 8.2–8.3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #practical-strategy #study-protocol




















































## Key Figures & Intellectual Lineage

> [!person] **John Sweller** (1946–present)** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> Educational psychologist at the University of New South Wales; originator of Cognitive Load Theory (1988). Sweller's foundational insight came from studying problem-solving in mathematics: students using means-ends analysis performed worse on subsequent transfer tasks than students working through worked examples, because the problem-solving strategy saturated working memory with subgoal management rather than schema formation. From this observation, CLT developed.
> 
> **Core contribution to this report:** The three-load framework; the worked-example effect; the redundancy effect; the expertise-reversal reappraisal (with Kalyuga, 2019).
> 
> **Key works:** Sweller, J. (1988). *Cognitive load during problem solving*. *Cognitive Science, 12*(2), 257–285. Sweller, J., van Merriënboer, J. J. G., & Paas, F. (1998). *Cognitive architecture and instructional design*. *Educational Psychology Review, 10*, 251–296. Sweller, J., van Merriënboer, J. J. G., & Paas, F. (2019). Cognitive architecture and instructional design: 20 years later. *Educational Psychology Review, 31*, 261–292.

> [!person] **Alan Baddeley** (1934–present)** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> British cognitive psychologist; developer (with Graham Hitch) of the influential multi-component model of working memory (1974), replacing Atkinson and Shiffrin's unitary short-term memory store with a three-then-four component architecture. Baddeley's model provides the cognitive architecture that CLT's load analysis operates within.
> 
> **Core contribution to this report:** Multi-component WM model (Central Executive, phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, episodic buffer); working memory capacity as the binding constraint on learning and complex cognition.

> [!person] **Fred Paas** (contemporary)** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> Dutch educational psychologist; collaborator with Sweller; developer of the primary subjective measurement instrument for cognitive load — the 9-point mental effort rating scale (Paas, 1992). Paas's measurement methodology transformed CLT from a theoretical framework into an empirically testable research programme.
> 
> **Core contribution to this report:** Cognitive load measurement methodology; germane load operationalisation; systematic CLT research programme.

> [!person] **Jeroen van Merriënboer** (contemporary)** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> Educational technologist at Maastricht University; collaborator with Sweller and Paas; architect of the [[Four-Component-Instructional-Design]] (4C/ID) model — an application of CLT principles to complex professional skills training. Van Merriënboer's work extends CLT from simple task learning to complex, whole-task skill development.
> 
> **Core contribution to this report:** Germane load concept (with Sweller and Paas, 1998); extension of CLT to complex learning; practical CLT design guidelines.

> [!person] **Alan Baddeley (b. 1934) & Graham Hitch (b. 1947) — Cognitive Psychology, York/Sussex/Manchester** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Core Contribution:** Replaced the unitary short-term store of Atkinson-Shiffrin with a multi-component working memory model (1974). Distinguished phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and central executive; later added episodic buffer (Baddeley, 2000). Provided the foundational architecture against which all subsequent PKM cognitive analysis is conducted.
> **Key Work:** Baddeley & Hitch (1974). "Working Memory." In G. Bower (Ed.), *The Psychology of Learning and Motivation* (Vol. 8).
> **Relationship to others:** Extended Atkinson & Shiffrin (1968); in dialogue with Cowan (2001) on capacity estimates.

> [!person] **Nelson Cowan (b. 1951) — Cognitive Psychology, University of Missouri** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Core Contribution:** Embedded-processes model of working memory that downwardly revised capacity estimates from Miller's 7±2 to 4±1 chunks (2001). Clarified that the "chunks" whose activation counts are episodic, context-dependent units of meaning, not fixed-size storage slots.
> **Key Work:** Cowan, N. (2001). "The magical number 4 in short-term memory: A reconsideration of mental storage capacity." *Behavioral and Brain Sciences, 24*, 87–114.
> **Relationship to others:** Engaged critically with Miller (1956); refined and constrained Baddeley-Hitch model.

> [!person] **Andy Clark (b. 1957) & David Chalmers (b. 1966) — Philosophy, Edinburgh/ANU** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Core Contribution:** Extended Mind Thesis (1998): cognitive processes can extend beyond the brain when external resources satisfy functional equivalence and coupling conditions. Provided the philosophical scaffolding for treating the PKB as a constituent part of the practitioner's cognitive system, not merely a tool.
> **Key Work:** Clark, A., & Chalmers, D. (1998). "The extended mind." *Analysis, 58*(1), 7–19.
> **Relationship to others:** Clark extended his earlier work in being-in-the-world (1997); Chalmers brought consciousness philosophy; joint work influenced Hutchins' distributed cognition empirically.

> [!person] **John Sweller (b. 1946) — Educational Psychology, UNSW Sydney** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Core Contribution:** Cognitive Load Theory (1988, substantially developed 1994–present). Operationalised the working memory constraint for instructional design; distinguished intrinsic, extraneous, and germane load; generated substantial empirical research on worked examples, split-attention effects, expertise reversal.
> **Key Work:** Sweller, J. (1988). "Cognitive load during problem solving: Effects on learning." *Cognitive Science, 12*, 257–285.
> **Relationship to others:** Built on Baddeley-Hitch working memory architecture; extended by Paas & van Merriënboer.

> [!person] **Frédéric Vallée-Tourangeau — Cognitive Science, Kingston University London** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Core Contribution:** Interactivity theory of cognition: thinking is constitutively interactive when practitioners physically manipulate problem representations. Demonstrated that providing physical manipulation opportunities substantially improved problem-solving performance in arithmetic and insight problems, even when total information available was held constant.
> **Key Work:** Vallée-Tourangeau, F. (2013). "Interactivity, efficiency, and individual differences in mental arithmetic." *Experimental Psychology, 60*(4), 302–311.
> **Relationship to others:** Extended Kirsh & Maglio on epistemic actions; complementary to Clark & Chalmers at the empirical level.

> [!person] **Frederic Bartlett (1886–1969)** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> British psychologist; pioneer of schema theory and the constructive nature of memory. His 1932 "Remembering" demonstrated through the "War of the Ghosts" experiments that memory is reconstructive, not reproductive, and that schemas derived from cultural and experiential background systematically shape recall. Key work: *Remembering* (1932). Relationship to other figures: preceded Piaget's formal schema theory timeline; Rumelhart and later computational researchers formalized and extended Bartlett's insight.
> 
> **See also:** [[Frederic-Bartlett]], [[Schema-Theory]], [[Schema-Theory-Bartlett,-Rumelhart]]

> [!person] **David Rumelhart (1942–2011)** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> American cognitive psychologist and AI researcher; provided the first rigorous computational specification of schemas. His connectionist work and schema theory contributions (1975–1984) formalized schemas as data structures with slots, defaults, and constraints. Co-developed the "interactive activation model" of reading and worked on parallel distributed processing. Key works: "Notes on a Schema for Stories" (1975), "Schemata: The Building Blocks of Cognition" (1980). Relationship: formalized Bartlett's insight; influenced Cognitive Load Theory researchers.
> 
> **See also:** [[David-Rumelhart]], [[schemas]], [[Schema-Theory]]

> [!person] **Andy Clark (1957–)** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> British philosopher of mind; primary architect of the extended mind thesis and the cognitive niche concept. His work traces the boundary extension of the mind through interactions with tools, language, and environment. Key works: "The Extended Mind" (with David Chalmers, 1998), *Being There* (1997), *Supersizing the Mind* (2008), *The Experience Machine* (2023). Relationship to Chalmers: joint architect of the Parity Principle; their paper sparked decades of philosophy of mind debate.
> 
> **See also:** [[Andy-Clark]], [[Extended-Mind-Theory]], [[Parity-Principle]], [[4E-Cognition]]

> [!person] **John Sweller (1940–)** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> Australian educational psychologist; founder of Cognitive Load Theory (CLT). His work on problem solving and learning established the foundational account of how working memory constraints govern learning through schema construction. Key works: "Cognitive Load During Problem Solving" (1988), *Cognitive Load Theory* (2011, with Kalyuga & others). Relationship to Baddeley: CLT applies Baddeley's working memory architecture to instructional design questions; relationship to Rumelhart: applies schema theory to learning theory.
> 
> **See also:** [[Cognitive-Load-Theory]], [[Working-Memory]], [[Schema-Theory]]

> [!person] **Alan Baddeley (1934–)** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> British cognitive psychologist; architect of the working memory model (with Graham Hitch, 1974). Replaced the unitary short-term memory concept with a multicomponent working memory system. Key additions: the episodic buffer (2000). Key works: *Working Memory* (1986), *Your Memory: A User's Guide* (1982), "The Concept of Working Memory" (Baddeley & Hitch, 1974). Relationship to Miller: extended and refined Miller's capacity framework; relationship to Sweller: provided the architectural basis for CLT.
> 
> **See also:** [[Alan-Baddeley]], [[Baddeley's-Working-Memory-Model]], [[Working-Memory-Capacity]]

> [!person] **Hermann Ebbinghaus (1850–1909)** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> German psychologist who produced the first systematic empirical study of memory (1885). Originator of the forgetting curve and founding documentation of the spacing effect. His self-experiments established that distributed practice is far more efficient than massed practice — a finding that has been replicated in every domain and population tested since.
> 
> **Relationship to report:** The foundational empiricist whose spacing data underpins all modern spaced repetition systems. Methodologically pioneering (quantitative memory science); his findings are more practically useful today than when published, because computational SRS can now implement optimal spacing at scale.

> [!person] **Robert Bjork (born 1939)** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> UCLA cognitive psychologist who introduced the [[Desirable-Difficulties]] framework and conducted foundational research on spacing, interleaving, and the distinction between storage strength and retrieval strength. Arguably the single most influential figure in the translation of cognitive psychology to evidence-based education.
> 
> **Relationship to report:** The Desirable Difficulties framework is the unifying theoretical lens through which all seven strategies in this report are interpreted. Bjork's distinction between conditions that enhance *current performance* versus conditions that enhance *long-term learning* is the most practically important single insight in learning science.

> [!person] **John Dunlosky (born 1968)** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> Kent State cognitive psychologist whose 2013 meta-review (with Rawson, Marsh, Nathan, and Willingham) evaluated the effectiveness and utility of ten commonly used learning techniques. This review provides the highest-level evidence base for the strategy recommendations in this report.
> 
> **Relationship to report:** The Dunlosky et al. (2013) "high utility" designations for retrieval practice and distributed practice, combined with the "low utility" designation for re-reading and highlighting, are the primary evidence-based justification for the strategy selection in this report.

> [!person] **Michelene Chi (born c. 1950)** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> Carnegie Mellon cognitive and learning scientist best known for her documentation of the self-explanation effect and her ICAP framework (Interactive-Constructive-Active-Passive). Her work on expert-novice differences in problem solving established that experts self-explain more spontaneously and more effectively than novices.
> 
> **Relationship to report:** The [[Self-Explanation-Effect]] is a key component of Section 6, and Chi's documentation of its mechanisms (gap-detection, mental model construction) directly informs the explanation note format and the self-explanation prompts in the reading protocol.

> [!person] **Edward Deci and Richard Ryan (born 1942 and 1953)** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> University of Rochester psychologists who developed [[Self-Determination-Theory]] from the early 1970s through its current comprehensive formulation. Their experimental programs on intrinsic vs. extrinsic motivation, basic psychological needs, and the internalization continuum constitute the most empirically developed motivation theory in psychology.
> 
> **Relationship to report:** SDT provides the motivational architecture (Section 8) that converts the cognitive strategies from intellectually interesting techniques into components of a sustainable reading life.

> [!person] **Barry Zimmerman (1942–2023)** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> CUNY Graduate Center educational psychologist who developed the most influential model of self-regulated learning, including the cyclical forethought-performance-self-reflection model. His work on self-efficacy and SRL bridged Bandura's social cognitive theory with educational application.
> 
> **Relationship to report:** Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model provides the temporal scaffold (Section 10) that sequences the seven strategies into a coherent before-during-after workflow.

> [!person] **Alan Baddeley (1934–)** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> *University of York, UK*
> Architect of the multicomponent [[Baddeley's-Working-Memory-Model|working memory model]] (1974, 2000) that provides the cognitive architecture framework for this report. His model of the central executive, phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and episodic buffer defines the resource pools that walking and reading compete for.
> **Key work cited:** Baddeley, A.D. (2000). *The episodic buffer: A new component of working memory?* Trends in Cognitive Sciences, 4(11), 417-423.
> **Relationship to others:** Built on Atkinson-Shiffrin's modal model; influenced Sweller's CLT; disagreements with Cowan regarding unitarity of WM.

> [!person] **John Sweller (1946–)** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> *University of New South Wales, Australia*
> Creator of [[Cognitive-Load-Theory]] (1988), the framework that explains why any secondary task can degrade learning from complex material by consuming finite working memory resources.
> **Key work cited:** Sweller, J. (2011). *Cognitive Load Theory*. Springer.
> **Relationship to others:** Built directly on Baddeley's WM model and Miller's capacity limits; extended by Paas, van Merriënboer, and Kalyuga.

> [!person] **Christopher Wickens (1938–2020)** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> *University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign*
> Developer of [[Multiple-Resource-Theory]] (1984, 2002), which predicts that walking and reading show moderate interference because they overlap primarily on central executive demands while using largely distinct perceptual, coding, and response resources.
> **Key work cited:** Wickens, C.D. (2008). *Multiple resources and mental workload.* Human Factors, 50(3), 449-455.
> **Relationship to others:** Complemented and challenged Pashler's single-bottleneck theory; influenced applied human factors research.

> [!person] **Harold Pashler** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> *University of California, San Diego*
> Leading proponent of the [[Psychological-Refractory-Period|central bottleneck theory]] of dual-task interference (1994), providing the most stringent theoretical predictions about when concurrent tasks must queue for central processing.
> **Key work cited:** Pashler, H. (1994). *Dual-task interference in simple tasks: Data and theory.* Psychological Bulletin, 116(2), 220-244.
> **Relationship to others:** Challenged by Wickens' multiple resource approach; built on Welford's refractory period work.

> [!person] **Norman J. Slamecka (1930–2003)** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> *University of Toronto*
> Discoverer of the [[Generation-Effect|generation effect]] (1978), the finding that established active generation as superior to passive reception for memory — the decisive principle in this report's practical recommendations.
> **Key work cited:** Slamecka, N.J., & Graf, P. (1978). *The generation effect: Delineation of a phenomenon.* Journal of Experimental Psychology: Human Learning and Memory, 4(6), 592-604.
> **Relationship to others:** Influenced Bjork's desirable difficulties framework; extended by Bertsch et al. (2007).

> [!person] **Robert A. Bjork** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> *University of California, Los Angeles*
> Developer of the [[Desirable-Difficulties|desirable difficulties]] framework (1994), which provides the broader theoretical context for the generation effect and explains why effortful learning strategies — despite feeling harder — produce superior long-term retention.
> **Key work cited:** Bjork, R.A. (1994). *Memory and metamemory considerations in the training of human beings.* In J. Metcalfe & A. Shimamura (Eds.), Metacognition. MIT Press.
> **Relationship to others:** Extended Slamecka's generation effect; influenced educational reform toward testing-based learning; wife Elizabeth Bjork as collaborator.



























## Open Questions

> [!open-question] **The Long-Term Schema and PKM Evolution Question** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> As a practitioner's schemas develop over years of PKM practice, does the system's role fundamentally change — from a cognitive extension that supplements limited internal storage to a cognitive mirror that reinforces and expands rich internal schemas? And if so, what design features support this evolution? No research has tracked the cognitive load profile of an individual PKM practitioner longitudinally across a decade or more of expertise development. The expertise-reversal principle predicts that such evidence would show a U-shaped relationship between system complexity and cognitive benefit: increasing complexity benefits novices up to a point, then reverses. But the location of the reversal point and the characteristics that predict it remain empirically uncharted.

> [!open-question] **The Coupling Threshold: When Does External Become Cognitive?** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> The Clark-Chalmers coupling conditions (reliability, trust, prior endorsement) define qualitative criteria for genuine cognitive extension, but they do not specify quantitative thresholds. How reliable is reliably functional? How endorsed is "prior endorsement"? The philosophical literature has not provided operationally testable criteria, and the empirical cognitive science has not systematically investigated the conditions under which practitioners treat external resources as trusted cognitive components versus external tools requiring verification. This question has significant practical implications for PKM design: it determines what quality standards are constitutively required for genuine cognitive extension versus merely useful information retrieval.

> [!open-question] **Open Question: Direct Empirical Evidence for Bidirectional Enrichment** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> The Bidirectional Enrichment Hypothesis is well-motivated by convergent research (self-explanation, generation effect, expertise development, constructivist memory) but has not been directly tested in PKB contexts. Key empirical questions include: (1) Do PKB practitioners who maintain high schema-fidelity systems show measurably different internal schema development trajectories than those who use low-fidelity systems? (2) Is there evidence that external schema structure reshapes internal organization over time, or does the influence run only inside-out? (3) What behavioral markers distinguish PKB use that is driving the enrichment cycle from PKB use that is falling into the substitution trap? These are empirically tractable questions that current research has not addressed.

> [!open-question] **Open Question 1: Optimal Note Density** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> What is the optimal density of note-making during reading? Research suggests that more active note-making generally produces better learning — but at some point, excessive note-making may fragment reading-flow, reduce attention to the text, and create elaborations of peripheral rather than central points. The point of diminishing returns — and whether it varies by learner expertise, text type, and learning goal — is not well established empirically.

> [!open-question] **Open Question 2: AI-Augmented PKB and Deep Learning** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> The integration of AI assistance in PKB workflows (automated link suggestion, note generation, flashcard creation) raises the question: at what level of automation does the cognitive effort required for deep encoding disappear, converting the PKB back into a passive repository? The generation effect depends on the learner doing the generating; outsourcing generation to AI may undermine the primary encoding mechanism while appearing to accelerate information processing.

> [!open-question] **Individual Differences in Dual-Task Tolerance** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> How much do individual differences in [[Working-Memory-Capacity|working memory capacity]], [[Executive-Function|executive function]], and physical fitness modulate the dual-task cost of walking while reading? High-WMC individuals may have sufficient "executive slack" to absorb walking's costs with minimal comprehension impact. The literature suggests this is possible but has not been directly tested for the walking-reading combination.







## Protocols & Methods

> [!protocol] **Protocol 1: Element Interactivity Analysis for Instructional Design** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-deep-dive-2026-04-12]])*
> **Purpose:** Estimate the element interactivity of learning materials to guide instructional format decisions.
> **Steps:**
> 1. **Identify the target learner population** and estimate their schema development for the content domain.
> 2. **Decompose the material** into elements at a grain size appropriate to the learner's expertise level. For novices, elements are individual symbols, facts, or steps. For intermediates, elements are concept clusters. For experts, elements are schema-level abstractions.
> 3. **Map the interactions:** Which elements must be processed simultaneously? What happens if any element is processed in isolation? Draw the interaction structure.
> 4. **Estimate effective element interactivity:** How many interactions remain after the target learner's schemas have chunked familiar patterns?
> 5. **Compare to capacity threshold:** If effective EI > ~4-5 simultaneous interactions, the material WILL produce overload for the target population.
> 6. **Design response:**
>    - If effective EI is too high: Sequence using isolated-interacting principle, provide worked examples, reduce extraneous format interactivity, or provide additional scaffolding.
>    - If effective EI is below threshold: Check for expertise reversal — can scaffolding be removed? Are materials too simple for the target population?
> 
> **Limitations:** Step 2 requires domain expertise and judgment; no standard grain size exists. Step 4 is estimative, not precise.

> [!protocol] **Protocol 2: Progressive Magnification Study Method** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-deep-dive-2026-04-12]])*
> **Purpose:** Achieve specialist-level understanding of any narrow topic through systematic depth progression.
> **Steps:**
> 1. **Narrow the topic** until you can name a single mechanism, construct, or phenomenon.
> 2. **Surface (Level 1):** What does this topic appear to be? What do introductory sources say? What mental model does a non-specialist carry?
> 3. **Mechanism (Level 2):** How does this actually work? What is the underlying mechanism that generates the surface appearance?
> 4. **Substructure (Level 3):** What are the components of the mechanism? How are they defined? What assumptions are hidden in the technical terms?
> 5. **Dynamics (Level 4):** How do the components interact to produce the observable behavior? Can you derive the surface-level phenomena from the substructure?
> 6. **Edge Cases (Level 5):** Where does the standard understanding break down? What are the boundary conditions and exceptions?
> 7. **Frontier (Level 6):** What are researchers currently trying to figure out? Where is the active debate?
> 8. **Speculation (Level 7):** If current research programs succeed, what would the field look like? What informed extrapolations can you make?
> 
> **Quality check:** Each level must go DEEPER than the previous, not sideways. If you find yourself covering new aspects at the same depth, merge or restructure.

> [!protocol] **Protocol 1: The Load Phase Audit** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> A quarterly diagnostic to identify where cognitive load is being allocated in your PKM workflow.
> 
> **Step 1: Map your PKM activities to load phases.**
> List all activities you perform in operating your PKM system. For each, identify:
> - **Primary load type**: Extraneous (maintenance, navigation, filing decisions) / Intrinsic (comprehension, complex synthesis) / Germane (connection-making, elaboration, retrieval practice)
> - **Estimated weekly time investment**
> 
> **Step 2: Calculate load-type ratios.**
> Tally the weekly time in each category. Healthy ratios approximate:
> - Extraneous (maintenance): <20% of total PKM time
> - Intrinsic (comprehension): 30–40%
> - Germane (synthesis/retrieval): >40%
> 
> **Step 3: Identify the top extraneous load source.**
> Which single activity, if eliminated or automated, would most reduce maintenance overhead?
> 
> **Step 4: Identify the germane load gap.**
> Which synthesis or retrieval activities are you currently not doing that the research evidence most strongly supports? (Common gaps: write-from-memory summaries, active retrieval of key frameworks, deliberate connection identification.)
> 
> **Step 5: Design one change.**
> Implement a single structural change to address the top extraneous source or fill the most critical germane gap. Evaluate after four weeks.
> 
> **Frequency:** Quarterly, or whenever you feel your PKM system is overwhelming rather than supporting your thinking.

> [!protocol] **Protocol 2: The Write-From-Memory Synthesis Protocol** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> A structured approach to knowledge synthesis that forces germane processing by replacing passive review with active reconstruction.
> 
> **When to use:** After reading a book, completing a course unit, or processing a significant cluster of related notes.
> 
> **Steps:**
> 1. **Close all sources.** Do not refer to your notes, highlights, or the original material.
> 2. **Write for 15 minutes**, answering: "What do I now understand about [topic] that I did not understand before? What connections does this create with what I already knew?"
> 3. **Identify gaps.** Note where your reconstruction is uncertain, missing, or confused.
> 4. **Review sources for gaps only.** Return to original material only for the specific gaps identified.
> 5. **Write a connection note.** Identify 3 specific links between the new material and existing notes in your PKB. Write a note capturing each connection — not just the link, but what the two concepts illuminate in each other.
> 
> **Why it works:** Recall without cues forces retrieval of the most robustly encoded material; gaps reveal what needs reinforcement; connection identification drives elaborative encoding at the integration level.

> [!protocol] **Protocol 3: Minimum Viable Connection Requirement** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> A linking discipline that ensures new notes are integrated into the knowledge graph rather than isolated.
> 
> **Rule:** No note is complete until it has been explicitly connected to at least two existing notes with **bidirectional links** and a **connection sentence** explaining *why* the connection is meaningful — not just that it exists.
> 
> **Connection sentence template:** "[Note A] relates to [Note B] because [specific insight about their relationship], which implies [practical or theoretical consequence for understanding either concept]."
> 
> **Example:** "[[Expertise-Reversal-Effect]] relates to [[Desirable-Difficulties]] because both concern conditions under which instructional supports that reduce apparent difficulty degrade rather than enhance learning — which implies that optimal PKM design must assess the learner's current schema state, not simply minimise friction."
> 
> **Why it works:** Forces elaborative encoding of each connection; builds a retrievable knowledge graph rather than isolated captures; the act of writing the connection sentence often generates the most valuable insight from a reading or synthesis session.

> [!protocol] **Protocol 1: The Schema Audit — Assessing Your PKB's Schema-Fidelity** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Purpose:** Assess current PKB against the five schema-fidelity properties.
> 
> **Steps:**
> 1. **Relational primacy check:** Select 20 notes at random. Count the proportion with typed or descriptically specific links vs. bare wiki-links or no links. Target: >60% typed/specific.
> 2. **Hierarchical stratification check:** Identify your MOCs or index notes. Trace from MOC → subtopic hub → individual notes. Can you move from broad to specific at 3+ levels? Target: yes for major knowledge domains.
> 3. **Associative density check:** Average number of links per note. Target: >5 meaningful links per note for developed areas.
> 4. **Categorical coherence check:** Within any single tag or folder cluster, are notes genuinely related by underlying conceptual category, or merely by surface topic? Identify any cluster with >30% tangential members.
> 5. **Activation pathway check:** Can you reach most notes from 2-3 central hub notes in ≤3 hops? Target: yes for knowledge areas you work in regularly.
> 
> **Output:** Schema-fidelity diagnostic profile across five dimensions. Identify lowest-scoring dimension as priority area.

> [!protocol] **Protocol 2: The Interleaved Review Practice** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Purpose:** Actively counteract the fluency illusion by using the PKB for retrieval testing rather than passive review.
> 
> **Steps:**
> 1. Select a knowledge area for review (not for adding new content — specifically for review).
> 2. Close all notes. From memory only, write out the key concepts, their relationships, and the structural organization of the area.
> 3. Open the PKB and compare your written reconstruction against the actual notes.
> 4. Note: (a) what you included correctly, (b) what you mis-reconstructed, (c) what you omitted entirely.
> 5. For each omission or error, rebuild the note structure — don't just re-read; actively reconstruct.
> 6. Repeat for the same area in 1 week. Measure improvement in reconstruction accuracy.
> 
> **Target frequency:** Once per month per major knowledge area. More frequent for areas in active development.

> [!protocol] **Protocol 3: The Elaborative Link Practice** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Purpose:** Convert bare wiki-links into typed, elaborative links that support schema construction rather than mere navigation.
> 
> **Steps:**
> 1. Identify any note with ≥3 bare wiki-links (links that are just `[[concept]]` without relationship specification).
> 2. For each bare link, add a brief explanatory clause: e.g., `[[Schema-Theory|schemas]], which explain how prior knowledge structures organize encoding`.
> 3. When creating new notes, adopt the discipline of never adding a bare link — always specify the relationship in context.
> 4. For the PKB as a whole, consider implementing typed link conventions (e.g., "because," "enables," "requires," "contrasts-with") as syntactic sugar for relationship specification.
> 
> **Target:** Zero bare links in any note more than 3 months old.

> [!protocol] **The Complete Strategic Reading Session Protocol** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Before Reading (10–15 minutes):**
> - [ ] Identify learning goal: What should you understand/be able to do after reading?
> - [ ] Activate prior knowledge: 5-minute free recall of what you already know about the topic
> - [ ] Review 2–3 related PKB notes for schema warm-up
> - [ ] Generate 3–5 advance questions from headings and abstract
> 
> **During Reading (adapted to text length and density):**
> - [ ] Look-away note-making after each major subsection
> - [ ] Online comprehension monitoring (flag unclear passages with "?")
> - [ ] Self-explanation annotations for key claims ("this is true because...")
> - [ ] Generative connection notes ("this connects to [[Related-Concept]] because...")
> 
> **After Reading (15–20 minutes):**
> - [ ] Immediate free recall: write everything remembered without looking at notes
> - [ ] Compare recall to source text; identify gaps
> - [ ] Create/update atomic concept notes in PKB from recall + gap-filled source review
> - [ ] Build wiki-link connections for each new note (minimum 2 per note)
> - [ ] Mark flashcard seeds in notes for SRS queue
> - [ ] Metacognitive self-rating: comprehension depth (1–5), strategy effectiveness (1–5)

> [!protocol] **Multi-Pass Active Reading Protocol for PKB** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Pass 1 — Survey (5-10 minutes):** Skim the report structure: title, abstract, section headings, callouts, expansion topics. Activate relevant schemas. Identify connections to existing permanent notes. Set reading goals: "What do I need to learn from this?"
> 
> **Pass 2 — Deep Read with Annotation (30-45 minutes):** Read sequentially. Highlight key claims, definitions, and insights. Write marginal notes: "This connects to [[X]]," "This contradicts what I thought about Y," "Need to understand this mechanism better." Flag passages for PKB note creation.
> 
> **Pass 3 — PKB Processing (20-30 minutes):** Create or update permanent notes for key concepts. Write wiki-linked connections. Generate flashcard seeds. Update relevant MOCs. This is where the generation effect operates most powerfully — transforming passive annotations into active knowledge construction.
> 
> **Pass 4 — Consolidation Walk (15-20 minutes):** Walk while mentally reviewing what you learned. Try to explain the three most important ideas from memory. Notice where your understanding breaks down — these gaps become targets for re-reading.

> [!protocol] **Quick Decision Protocol: Walk or Read at Desk?** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> Before starting a reading session, ask three questions:
> 
> 1. **Is this material novel or review?**
>    - Novel (new concepts, unfamiliar frameworks) → DESK
>    - Review (re-reading familiar material) → Walking is acceptable
> 
> 2. **Is the element interactivity high or low?**
>    - High (multi-step arguments, interdependent concepts) → DESK
>    - Low (narrative, biographical, sequential) → Walking is acceptable
> 
> 3. **Will I actively process (annotate, note-make, PKB-link)?**
>    - Yes → DESK (active processing requires hands, tools, and focused attention)
>    - No → Walking is acceptable, but consider whether active processing would be more valuable

> [!protocol] **Walk-Read-Walk Sandwich Protocol** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Pre-Walk (15-20 min):** Walk at comfortable pace. Mentally preview: what do you already know about this topic? What questions do you bring? What permanent notes in your PKB relate? This activates schemas and primes encoding.
> 
> **Desk Reading (45-60 min):** Deep read with annotation → PKB processing → summarization. Apply the four-pass protocol from Section 8.3.
> 
> **Post-Walk (15-20 min):** Walk at comfortable pace. Attempt to recall the three most important ideas without notes. Identify where understanding breaks down. Generate creative connections to other PKB topics. These gaps and connections become next-session targets.

> ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

> │    Element interactivity as a background concept             │
> │           ↓ zoom                                            │

> [!diagram] **Argument Map 1: PKM Systems Generate Extraneous Load** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> ```
> PREMISE 1: PKM systems require ongoing maintenance
>     (tagging, filing, reviewing, updating hierarchies)
>         |
>         v
> PREMISE 2: Maintenance activities generate cognitive
>     load independent of content complexity
>         |
>         v
> PREMISE 3: Working memory capacity is shared between
>     maintenance and comprehension/synthesis tasks
>         |
>         v
> INTERMEDIATE CONCLUSION: Maintenance activities
>     consume working memory that would otherwise be
>     available for synthesis and connection-making
>         |
>         v
> PREMISE 4: Working memory resources allocated to
>     maintenance cannot simultaneously support
>     elaborative encoding or connection generation
>         |
>         v
> CONCLUSION: PKM systems create extraneous load
>     that directly reduces the cognitive bandwidth
>     available for the generative work they exist to support
> 
> COUNTERCLAIM: PKM systems can reduce load by
>     offloading memory to external storage
> RESPONSE: This is true for accessed material,
>     but only when access costs < reconstruction costs;
>     maintenance costs are ALWAYS extraneous and
>     cannot be offset by offloading benefits
> ```

> [!diagram] **Argument Map 2: Germane Processing Requires Effortful Engagement** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> ```
> EVIDENCE: Levels-of-processing research (Craik & Lockhart, 1972)
>     → Deeper processing → stronger memory traces
>         |
> EVIDENCE: Retrieval practice advantage (Karpicke & Roediger, 2008)
>     → Active recall outperforms passive re-study
>         |
> EVIDENCE: Generation effect
>     → Self-generated answers better retained than provided answers
>         |
>         v
> PRINCIPLE: Cognitive effort during encoding,
>     not passive exposure, drives schema formation
>         |
>         v
> APPLICATION TO PKM:
>     Smooth, frictionless capture produces weak encoding
>     Generative note-making produces strong encoding
>         |
>         v
> TENSION: Frictionless capture is widely promoted as
>     PKM best practice (zero-friction capture philosophy)
>         |
>         v
> RESOLUTION: Distinguish capture friction from
>     synthesis friction — reduce the former,
>     preserve and increase the latter
>         |
>         v
> DESIGN PRESCRIPTION: Capture can be frictionless;
>     synthesis must involve active generation,
>     connection-making, and retrieval under partial cues
> ```

> [!diagram] **The Cognitive Justification Chain for PKM as Extended Mind** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> ```
> Working Memory Constraint (empirically established)
>         │
>         ▼
> Cognitive Offloading Reduces Demand (Risko & Gilbert)
>         │
>         ├──► Motor Offloading ──► reduces procedural load
>         ├──► Perceptual Offloading ──► reduces pattern recognition load
>         └──► Computational Offloading ──► reduces maintenance/manipulation load
>                         │
>                         ▼
>              PKB as Computational Offloading Infrastructure
>                         │
>                         ▼
>         Extended Mind Coupling Conditions (Clark & Chalmers)
>         ├── Reliability (system always functional) ──► Principle 3 (Trust Condition)
>         ├── Accessibility (always available) ──► Principle 2 (Zero-Friction Capture)
>         └── Prior Endorsement (practitioner trusts it) ──► Principle 6 (Fidelity)
>                         │
>                         ▼
>         PKB constitutes genuine extended cognitive system
>         (when design principles are satisfied)
>                         │
>                         ▼
>         Epistemic Action Infrastructure
>         (PKB performs cognitive operations, not just storage)
> ```

> [!diagram] **The Substitution Trap vs. Generative Investment** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> ```
>         ┌─────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────────┐
>         │  CAPTURE EVENT  │────►│  Processing Path A   │
>         │ (idea/reference)│     │  (Low Friction)      │
>         └─────────────────┘     │  Copy-paste / record │
>                                 │  No reformulation    │
>                                 │  → Inbox accumulates │
>                  │              │  → Substitution Trap │
>                  │              └─────────────────────┘
>                  │
>                  └────────────►  Processing Path B
>                                 (Generative Investment)
>                                 Reformulate in own words
>                                 Compare with prior notes
>                                 Identify implications
>                                 → Permanent Note created
>                                 → Schema integration
>                                 → Long-Term Memory
>                                 → Extended Cognition
> ```

> [!diagram] **Core Argument Structure** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> ```
> FOUNDATION PREMISES
> ├── P1: Schemas are active, relational cognitive structures
> │     (Bartlett 1932, Rumelhart 1980)
> ├── P2: Schemas vary in richness; richer schemas = better processing
> │     (Expertise research: Ericsson, Chase & Simon)
> └── P3: External structures can couple with internal cognitive systems
>       (Clark & Chalmers 1998; Sparrow et al. 2011)
> 
>         ↓ ↓ ↓ (synthesis)
> 
> INTERMEDIATE CLAIM: Schema-Fidelity
> If an external PKB mirrors schema organization → high schema-fidelity
> ↓
> MAIN THESIS: A high schema-fidelity PKB participates in cognitive processing
>             in analogous ways to internal schematic LTM
> 
>         ↓ coupling quality matters for this participation
>         ↓
> 
> DESIGN CRITERION: Five structural properties for schema-fidelity:
>   1. Relational primacy
>   2. Hierarchical stratification
>   3. Associative density
>   4. Categorical coherence
>   5. Activation pathway design
> 
>         ↓ if achieved ↓
> 
> PREDICTED OUTCOME: Bidirectional Enrichment Hypothesis
>   INSIDE-OUT: Richer internal schemas → more accurate PKB construction
>   OUTSIDE-IN: PKB building → self-explanation → richer internal schemas
>   FEEDBACK: Compounding generative cycle over time
> 
>         ↓ risk ↓
> 
> COUNTERFACTUAL: Substitution pathway (Fluency illusion + Sparrow effect)
>   LOW schema-fidelity PKB → outsource content without external schema structure
>   → reduce retrieval effort → weaken encoding → reduce internal schema development
> ```

> [!diagram] **The Schema-Fidelity Spectrum** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> ```
> LOW SCHEMA-FIDELITY ←————————————————→ HIGH SCHEMA-FIDELITY
> 
> Flat folder hierarchy               Hierarchical schema nodes +
> Keyword tags only                   typed, semantically-rich links
> "Buckets of content"                Associative network with
> No explicit relations               meaningful relationships
> Search-optimized                    Structure-optimized
> 
> Cognitive analog: Filing cabinet    Cognitive analog: Mind map +
>                                     semantic memory activation
> 
> Risk: Substitution trap             Benefit: Schema reinforcement cycle
> ```

> [!diagram] **Core Argument Structure: Walking-Reading vs. Desk-Based Active Reading** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> ```
> QUESTION: Should you walk while reading dense academic reports?
> 
> ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
> │  EVIDENCE STREAM 1: DUAL-TASK COSTS                 │
> │  Walking imposes modest but real cognitive cost      │
> │  (~10-25% for complex material)                     │
> │  [Pashler 1994, Wickens 2008, Al-Yahya 2011]       │
> └──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘
>                        │
> ┌──────────────────────▼──────────────────────────────┐
> │  EVIDENCE STREAM 2: AUTOMATICITY LIMITS             │
> │  Walking is partially, not fully, automatic          │
> │  Gaze stabilization + turning = residual cost        │
> │  [Woollacott 2002, Yogev-Seligmann 2008]            │
> └──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘
>                        │
> ┌──────────────────────▼──────────────────────────────┐
> │  EVIDENCE STREAM 3: EMBODIED COGNITION              │
> │  Walking enhances creativity BUT NOT                 │
> │  convergent analytical comprehension                 │
> │  [Oppezzo & Schwartz 2014]                          │
> └──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘
>                        │
> ┌──────────────────────▼──────────────────────────────┐
> │  EVIDENCE STREAM 4: EXERCISE TIMING                 │
> │  Post-exercise > during-exercise for complex tasks   │
> │  [Lambourne & Tomporowski 2010, Chang 2012]         │
> └──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘
>                        │
>                        ▼
>     ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
>     │  CONVERGENT CONCLUSION                   │
>     │  Walking-reading has a modest net cost   │
>     │  for complex analytical material         │
>     └───────────────────┬─────────────────────┘
>                         │
>                         │   HOWEVER, this is NOT
>                         │   the decisive factor...
>                         │
> ┌───────────────────────▼─────────────────────────────┐
> │  THE DECISIVE FACTOR: OPPORTUNITY COST              │
> │  Active PKB engagement invokes the Generation       │
> │  Effect (d = 0.40-0.87) — incompatible with walking │
> │  [Slamecka 1978, Bjork 1994, Dunlosky 2013]        │
> └───────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┘
>                         │
>                         ▼
>     ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
>     │  RECOMMENDATION                          │
>     │  Temporal separation:                    │
>     │  Walk → Read at desk with PKB → Walk    │
>     │  (arousal)  (comprehension)  (consolidation) │
>     └─────────────────────────────────────────┘
> ```












## Schema Activations

> [!schema-activation] **Prior Knowledge Activation: What You Already Know** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> Think about the most demanding cognitive moments in your PKM practice. The inbox that grew faster than you could process it. The taxonomy restructuring decision that paralyzed you for a week. The synthesis note you've been "about to write" for months. The moment you opened your knowledge base looking for something specific and navigated the UI for ten minutes without finding it. If any of these resonate, you have already experienced extraneous [[Cognitive-Load]] generated by your own system.
> 
> This report assumes familiarity with [[Working-Memory]] as a limited-capacity processing resource (see [[Baddeley's-Working-Memory-Model]]), basic [[Metacognition]] as the ability to monitor one's own cognitive processes, and some direct personal experience with a PKM system. You do not need to be a cognitive psychologist — the framework will be built from the ground up. But you do need to be willing to interrogate your own knowledge management practices against unflattering empirical evidence.
> 
> The guiding question for this entire report: **Under what conditions does a personal knowledge base function as cognitive infrastructure, and under what conditions does it function as cognitive overhead — and how do we reliably achieve the former?**
> 
> Related foundations: [[Cognitive-Architecture]], [[Schema-Theory]], [[Self-Regulated-Learning]], [[Extended-Mind-Theory]]

> [!schema-activation] **Activating Prior Knowledge: What You Already Know About This Topic** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> Before reading, consider what you already understand about these connected areas in your PKB:
> 
> **Existing conceptual anchors this report builds upon:**
> - **[[Cognitive-Load-Theory]]**: Sweller's framework distinguishing intrinsic, extraneous, and germane load. This report reframes the entire domain of PKM as a systematic extraneous-load reduction strategy.
> - **[[Extended-Mind-Theory]]**: Clark and Chalmers' argument that cognition can extend beyond the skin/skull boundary when external resources satisfy certain functional criteria. This report applies that framework specifically to PKB design.
> - **[[Metacognition]]**: Flavell's framework for thinking about thinking. Cognitive offloading is, in a crucial sense, metacognitive architecture — the practitioner must monitor their own working memory states to offload strategically.
> - **[[Long-Term-Memory]]**: The primary destination for information that has been processed out of working memory. Understanding the working-memory–long-term-memory interface is essential for understanding why offloading works when it does.
> 
> **Advance organiser — the report's central argument in one sentence:**
> Working memory is a finite, fragile, and non-trainable-beyond-narrow-limits resource; [[Personal-Knowledge-Management]] systems, when designed with this constraint as the organising principle, function as externally distributed working memory that amplifies what a single biological mind can accomplish.
> 
> **Guiding question to hold throughout:** *To what degree is a well-designed PKB a genuine extension of your cognitive architecture, and what design decisions follow from that claim?*

> [!schema-activation] **Prior Knowledge Bridge** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> Before proceeding, locate the cognitive territory you already occupy on this topic.
> 
> You likely already know that memory is not a filing cabinet — that remembering is reconstructive, not reproductive, and that what we retrieve is shaped by what we expect and already know. You may have encountered [[Schema-Theory]] through Piaget's assimilation/accommodation framework, or through the classic Bartlett experiment in which English undergraduates distorted an Indigenous folk tale when recalling it days later. You may know that [[Personal-Knowledge-Management]] systems like [[Zettelkasten]] and [[Personal-Knowledge-Base]] are advocated as tools for thinking, not merely storage. These threads are connected — but the connection is rarely made explicit in PKM discourse, and its implications are more radical than the self-help framing usually suggests.
> 
> **Advance Organizer:** This report argues that the relationship between a PKB and the mind is not metaphorical or merely instrumental. It is structural: a well-built PKB can instantiate the organizational principles of [[Cognitive-Architecture]] in an external medium, creating the conditions for genuine cognitive extension rather than sophisticated filing. The difference turns on what we mean by "schema" — and grasping that difference is the work of the first three sections.
> 
> **Guiding Question to Hold:** *If your PKB were destroyed tomorrow, what would happen to your internal knowledge structures — and what does your answer reveal about whether you have been externalizing schemas or merely offloading facts?*
> 
> Related foundation notes: [[Schema-Theory]], [[Extended-Mind-Theory]], [[Cognitive-Architecture]], [[Cognitive-Load-Theory]]

> [!schema-activation] **Prior Knowledge Bridge — Activating Your Reading Strategy Schema** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> Before encountering this report's analytical framework, briefly survey the existing knowledge structures you bring to this terrain.
> 
> You may have encountered [[Spaced-Repetition]] through applications like Anki or Obsidian's SR plugins — the intuition that distributing practice across time improves retention better than cramming. You may vaguely know the [[Forgetting-Curve]] — that without rehearsal, memories decay according to a predictable exponential function first described by [[Hermann-Ebbinghaus]]. You likely have some sense of [[Metacognition]] as "thinking about your own thinking," and perhaps you recognize [[Self-Regulated-Learning]] as the disciplined, self-directed management of one's own learning processes. Perhaps you have experimented with retrieval through self-quizzing, or used elaborative questioning techniques that prompt "why?" after reading each major claim.
> 
> What you may lack is the integrating architecture — the theory of how these scattered intuitions cohere into a unified, implementable system. This report provides that architecture, situating each strategy within its cognitive science rationale and showing how a [[Personal-Knowledge-Base]] can serve as the implementation platform for the full system, sustained by a motivational architecture drawn from [[Self-Determination-Theory]] and [[Autonomy-Support]].
> 
> **Guiding Question for This Report:** *How can a PKB-based strategic reading system be designed to simultaneously optimize the cognitive processing required for durable knowledge construction AND sustain the motivational engagement required for consistent, compound academic reading practice over years of lifelong learning?*

> [!schema-activation] **Bridging to What You Already Know** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> You have already encountered the core mechanisms at work in this question through your reading of reports on [[Cognitive-Load-Theory]], [[Working-Memory]], [[Self-Regulated-Learning]], and [[Metacognition]]. The [[Baddeley's-Working-Memory-Model|Baddeley model]] established that working memory has multiple components with limited capacity. [[Cognitive-Load-Theory]] demonstrated that instructional design must manage three types of load. And your understanding of [[Desirable-Difficulties]] tells you that effortful processing — not comfort — drives durable learning. The question this report addresses is where walking falls in this landscape: is it a comfortable habit that reduces productive friction, or is it a legitimate cognitive support that keeps your processing system online for longer? The guiding question is: **Given what cognitive science tells us about the architecture of attention, the costs of dual-tasking, and the benefits of embodied processing, what is the empirically grounded optimal reading strategy for someone processing dense academic reports — and how should you design the transition if the evidence points toward desk-based active engagement?**






## Active Reading Prompts

> [!active-reading] **Active Reading Checkpoint 1** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> Before continuing, take two minutes to audit your own PKM system against the six extraneous load categories above. Identify the single largest source of extraneous load in your current system. Keep this in mind as you read Section 5 (How PKM Systems Fail), which will provide design-level analysis of these failure modes.

> [!active-reading] **Active Reading Checkpoint 3 — Design Audit Invitation** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> With these design principles in front of you, take five minutes to perform a rapid audit of your current PKM system:
> 
> - **P1 (Simplicity):** What is the single most complexity-generating element of your system that produces the least retrieval value?
> - **P2 (Separation):** Do you have a reliable mechanism for deferring structural decisions until a dedicated time, or do you resolve them synchronously during capture?
> - **P3 (Retrieval paths):** For your last ten notes, how many retrieval paths does each have (folder position, tags, links)? Is one path sufficient?
> - **P4 (Mandatory connections):** Is linking to existing notes required by your workflow, or optional? What proportion of new notes have no outgoing links?
> 
> Prioritise one improvement from each principle level before moving to the Appendix.



## Far Transfer Applications

> [!far-transfer] **Far Transfer: Element Interactivity in User Interface Design** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-deep-dive-2026-04-12]])*
